Email: priyadarshee.patra@gmail.com Background: Alcohol dependence syndrome impacts multiple aspects of life and negatively affects sexual functioning, leading to erectile dysfunction. Aim: Determine the prevalence of erectile dysfunction in males with alcohol dependence syndrome and its association with the severity of alcohol dependence. Materials and Methods : A descriptive, cross-sectional study with 78 male subjects diagnosed with alcohol dependence syndrome at a tertiary care hospital. Severity of dependence was assessed using the SADQ-C, and erectile dysfunction was measured with the IIEF. Results: Erectile dysfunction was common among individuals with alcohol dependence, with severity positively correlated with alcohol dependence. Unidentified sexual dysfunction may perpetuate alcohol dependence and result in poor response to de-addiction therapy. Conclusions: The prevalence of erectile dysfunction was significantly higher than in the general population, particularly in patients with severe alcohol dependence. This highlights the importance of addressing sexual dysfunction in motivational counseling for heavy drinkers. Key words: alcohol dependence, erectile dysfunction, alcohol abuse. 3/20: Endoxifen in a Case of Bipolar Disorder with Hyperammonaemia and Renal Impairment: A New Ray of Hope in Medically Compromised Psychiatric Patients Dr. Nikhil Gautum Department of Psychiatry, Assistant Professor, C.M.C Hospital, Ludhiana, Punjab, India. Email: nikhilgautamx@gmail.com Background: Medications for managing bipolar mania often cause adverse effects. This case highlights the use of novel medication endoxifen. Case Description: A 65-year-old male with bipolar disorder for 30 years presented with severe psychiatric symptoms. Increased lithium dose led to complications, including altered sensorium, reduced urine output, and elevated serum creatinine and ammonia levels. Lithium was replaced with oxcarbazepine, but symptoms persisted, and lab values worsened. All medications were stopped, and endoxifen 8mg/day was started. Lab parameters improved over a week, but circadian rhythm and agitated behavior continued. Discharged on endoxifen monotherapy (16mg/day), the patient showed marked clinical improvement within a week and remained in remission for 2 months without adverse effects. Conclusion: Endoxifen shows promise in managing bipolar mania in patients with medical co-morbidities like hyperammonaemia and renal impairment. Key words: endoxifen, bipolar disorder, hyperammonaemia, renal imparement. 4/23: Prevalence of Depression in Stroke Survivors and The Relationship Between Site of Lesion and Duration Of Stroke Onset On Poststroke Depression Dr Jesna T J 1 , Dr. Aiswarya 2 , Dr. Radhakrishnan (HOD) 3 Department of Psychiatry, Junior resident, Travancore Medical College, Kollam, Kerala, India. email: jesnamdy@gmail.com Background: Poststroke depression (PSD) significantly affects recovery in stroke survivors. Understanding its prevalence and associations with lesion location and stroke onset duration is crucial. Aims: Estimate depression prevalence in stroke survivors and examine relationships between lesion location, stroke onset duration, and PSD. Methods: Cross-sectional study with 108 stroke survivors. Depression was assessed using the MINI scale, lesion locations determined through imaging, and stroke onset duration categorized. Data were analyzed using chi-square tests. Results: Major depressive disorder (MDD) prevalence was 31.49%. Males constituted 68.51% of the cohort, with higher depression rates in older age groups (61-70 years: 41.67%). Significant association found between stroke duration and depression, with 48.15% experiencing stroke within 2 years. Left cortex lesions were significantly associated with higher depression prevalence (p &lt; 0.001). Conclusion: PSD is prevalent in nearly one-third of stroke survivors, particularly with left cortical lesions and stroke duration between 6 months to 2 years. Keywords: Poststroke depression, lesion site, stroke duration, left cortex, prevalence, MDD. 5/24: Perinatal Depression Physical Symptoms Assessment using PHQ9 Dr. Anupama Korlakunta Department of Psychiatry, Assistant professor, NA, Secunderabad, Telangana India. Email: anu_ug@yahoo.co.in Introduction: Perinatal depression affects 10-20% of women during pregnancy or up to 12 months postpartum. Untreated, it can lead to long-term consequences for the mother, child, and family. Aims and Objectives: Screen perinatal women for depression using PHQ-9. Assess the physical symptoms of perinatal depression using PHQ-9. Methods and Materials: A prospective, single-center cross-sectional study at GMCH, Secunderabad, with IEC approval. Pregnant and new mothers attending the Obstetrics and Gynecology outpatient ward were included (N=122). The Telugu version of the PHQ-9 was used to assess depression. Results: Minimal (90.2%), mild (5.7%), and moderate (2.5%) levels of perinatal depression were found. Significant associations were found between perinatal depression and locality (p=0.01), education (p=0.01), occupation (p=0.10), and husband’s age (p=0.10). Conclusion: Screening for depression using PHQ-9 in pregnant and nursing mothers is crucial for early intervention and treatment to alleviate problems related to perinatal depression. Keywords: perinatal depression, PHQ9, Pregnancy, depression. 6/26: Assessment of Eating Attitude and Psychological States among Undergraduate Students of a Tertiary Health Care Centre, Puducherry. Dr. Kavitha R. R 1 , Joyshree Bora 2 , Dr. Moushumi Purkayastha Mukherjee 3 1.Department of Psychiatry, Tutor, College of Nursing, JIPMER, Puducherry, India. Email: Kavirr80@gmail.com Introduction: Healthcare students are prone to abnormal eating patterns due to their new environment and dynamic educational needs. Aims and Objective: Assess eating attitudes and psychological states among undergraduate students at tertiary health care centers and their association with demographic variables. Methods: Cross-sectional study with 325 undergraduate students (aged 18-25) at South Indian Medical College, selected via convenience sampling. Socio-demographic data, eating attitudes, and psychological states were assessed using a standardized questionnaire and analyzed with SPSS 21. Results: 11.1% of participants had abnormal eating attitudes. Mild to severe levels of depression and anxiety were found in 52.6% and 72.4% of participants, respectively, Eating attitudes were associated with depression, anxiety, stress, and demographic factors, and positively correlated with psychological states. Conclusion: Although eating attitudes were generally good, eating behaviors were concerning, highlighting the need for targeted mental health support and interventions. Keywords: eating attitudes, psychological states, depression, anxiety and stress 7/27: Psychiatric Aspects of Mass Datura Poisoning Dr Shashidhar Bhat 1 , Brig (Dr) P S Bhat (Retd) 2 , Dr Neeraj Zambare 3 , Dr Nisha Anna George 3 , Dr Anil Korade 4 , AVM (Dr) Rajvir Bhalwar (Retd) 5 1.Department of Psychiatry, Professor, Pravara Institute of Medical Sciences, Ahmednagar, Maharashtra, India. Email: pookalabhat@gmail.com Background: Deadly Nightshade (Atropa belladonna) contains toxic compounds causing anticholinergic toxicity, potentially leading to acute psychosis or delirium. Case Summary: Fifty individuals experienced severe symptoms after a marriage dinner and were treated at Pravara Rural Hospital. Symptoms included giddiness, blurred vision, sweating, headaches, fearfulness, and dry mouth. Physical exams revealed tachycardia, mydriasis, and urinary retention. Psychiatric consultations found anxiety, ataxia, disorientation, and visual hallucinations. Treatment involved hydration, short-acting benzodiazepines, and psychiatric counseling. All patients recovered within 36-48 hours and were discharged within two days. Discussion: Anticholinergic effects from various compounds can mimic acute psychosis, but deadly nightshade intoxication, while severe, showed no permanent effects, with higher severity in children and elderly. Conclusion: Mass Datura poisoning presents unique neuropsychiatric challenges, emphasizing the need for integrated psychiatric care in treatment protocols for accurate diagnosis and improved outcomes. Aims and Objectives: Study socio-demographic characteristics, clinical variables, the role of stressors, and phenomenology in ATPD patients. Methods: Cross-sectional observational study with 150 ATPD patients diagnosed per ICD-10 criteria. Information was recorded on a socio-demographic and clinical profile sheet. PSLES and PANSS scales were used to assess stressors and phenomenology. Results: Most patients were females aged 15-35 years (mean age: 32.45), educated, married, unemployed, living in a nuclear family, and from a rural background. Most presented within two weeks of illness onset without positive psychiatric family/past history. Three-fourths had stressors preceding illness onset. PANSS values were higher in females. Conclusions: Psychological stressors, typically occurring two weeks before illness onset, were prevalent. Life events should be considered triggers when making treatment decisions Key words: Acute, Transient, Psychotic disorders, PANSS 9/29: Benzodiazepines In Psychiatry Practice: Boon Or Bane ? Dr. Mohan Reddy M Dr. Stuti J. Mashru Sri Devaraj Urs Medical College, Tamaka, Kolar Background: Benzodiazepines, introduced in the mid-20th century, revolutionized psychiatric treatment but have addictive potential. History: First discovered by Leo Sternbach, Librium (chlordiazepoxide) was marketed in 1960, followed by Valium (diazepam) in 1963. Uses: Benzodiazepines treat generalized anxiety disorder, panic disorder, major depressive disorder, insomnia, bipolar disorder, schizophrenia, catatonia, seizure disorders, acute agitation, alcohol withdrawal, neuroleptic malignant syndrome, anesthesia, and various mental and neurological disorders by enhancing GABA effectiveness. Adverse Effects: Include sedation, cognitive and psychomotor impairment, dementia aggravation, disinhibition, abuse, and dependence. Long-term use can lead to tolerance, dependence, withdrawal, and overmedication symptoms. Conclusion: Despite adverse effects, benzodiazepines are crucial for managing psychiatric illnesses. Authors suggest cautious use, with monitoring and tapering for those at risk of dependence. NICE guidelines recommend use for no more than 4 weeks, but authors believe 3 months with gradual tapering is acceptable. Key words: Benzodiazepines, Boon, Bane. 10/40: Integrating Older People’s Mental Health into Primary Care – Lessons from the UK Experience Dr. Kallur Suresh Department of Psychiatry, Essex Partnership University NHS Foundation Trust, Bishops Stortford, Hertfordshire, United Kingdom. Email: kalsur40@hotmail.com Background: The aging population is increasing the demand for older people’s mental health services. Aims and Methods: Future care for older adults, particularly dementia diagnosis, will face capacity challenges. Integrating dementia care into primary settings can help manage this demand. Results and Discussion: In Essex, UK, a dementia care pathway involves local GPs and secondary care. Initial assessments for memory problems are done in secondary care, with MRI or CT scans requested. GPs handle ongoing care for stable patients, while a specialist neuro-cognitive clinic addresses complex cases. This collaboration improves dementia diagnosis rates and access to care. Conclusion: The Essex model showcases effective collaboration between hospitals and GPs to enhance dementia care and diagnosis, ensuring timely access to treatment. Key words: mental health, primary care, older people, dementia. 11/47: A cross-sectional study on burden and coping strategies in caregivers of patients with psychiatric illness Harshith Mahadev 1 , Deepak R S 2 , Pavitra K S 3 , Ananthkalyan 4 1.Department of Psychiatry, Basveshwara Medical College and Hospital, Chitradurga, Karnataka, India. Email: harshithmahadev@gmail.com Background: Primary caregivers of individuals with psychiatric disorders face significant emotional and physical burdens, affecting their mental well-being and quality of life. Aims: Assess caregiver burden related to specific psychiatric disorders. Assess coping strategies in primary caregivers. Methods: Cross-sectional study with 70 primary caregivers at Basveshwara Medical College and Hospital. Data on demographics were analyzed. The Zarit Burden Interview assessed burden, and the Brief Cope Scale evaluated coping strategies. Statistical analysis included descriptive statistics, ANOVA, and multivariate regression. Results: Caregivers had a mean age of 42.6 years, with 60% female and 55% spouses. Tailored support programs can improve caregiver resilience and overall well-being. Key words: quality of life, stress, schizophrenia, bipolar disorder, caregiver. 12/51: A Case Report of Amisulpride induced Prolactinoma Dr Harshali Tayade 1 , Dr Avinash Karde, Dr Ivan Netto 1.Department of psychiatry, Junior resident- BJGMC, Sass Suyog colony, Hivarkheda road, Jammer, Maharashtra, India. Email: dr22372tayade@gmail.com Introduction: Amisulpride is a potent dopamine D2 receptor blocking atypical antipsychotic that can cause prolactinoma. Case Details: A 30-year-old female with a history of suspiciousness, aggressive behavior, and hearing unreal voices was treated with amisulpride 200mg. She developed amenorrhea, galactorrhea, and hot flushes, leading to a diagnosis of prolactinoma (serum prolactin 199ng/ml; MRI showed a 3mm pituitary microadenoma). Despite stopping the medication, her symptoms persisted. She was later treated with aripiprazole, which reduced her prolactin levels to 48ng/ml, and her MRI showed no significant abnormalities. Conclusion: Clinicians should monitor prolactin levels in patients treated with amisulpride to detect hyperprolactinemia early and rule out other causes. Key words: Amisulpride, prolactinoma, galactorrhea, hyperprolactinemia. 13/54: A Cross Sectional Study Comparing The Severity of Mood and Anxiety Disorders in Individuals With and Without Adverse Childhood Experiences. Keerthi G 1 , Pavitra K S 2 , R S Deepak 3 , AnanthKalyan D 4 1.Department of psychiatry, Junior resident, Basaveshwara hospital and research centre, chitradurga, Mavelikkara, Tazhakara, kerala, India. Email: keerthiunnithan8@gmail.com Background: Adverse Childhood Experiences (ACEs) like abuse, neglect, and household dysfunction are risk factors for adult psychiatric disorders. Their specific impact on mood and anxiety disorder severity needs more research. Aims and Objectives: Compare depression, anxiety, and BPAD severity in individuals with and without ACEs and Examine effects of individual versus cumulative ACEs on symptom intensity. Methodology: 150 participants (67 with depression, 59 with anxiety disorders, and 24 with BPAD) were assessed for ACEs using the International ACE Questionnaire and severity using HAM-D, HAM-A, and YMRS scales. Results: Those with ACEs had significantly higher HAM-D, HAM-A, and YMRS scores. Emotional abuse and household substance abuse predicted higher HAM-D scores. Emotional abuse, physical neglect, and domestic violence predicted higher HAM-A scores. Household dysfunction was linked to higher YMRS scores. Participants with 3+ ACEs had higher severity scores across all disorders. Conclusion: ACEs significantly predict symptom severity in mood and anxiety disorders. Integrating ACE screening and trauma-informed care is recommended. Further research should explore protective factors. Key words: Depression, anxiety, adverse child experiences. 14/55: Personality Disorder is a Disease or Myth Dr. Jishnu Bhattacharya Department of psychiatry, Suri specialty hospital, Birbhum, 193 Andul road, Howrah, India. Email: drjishnu786@gmail.com Introduction: Personality disorders (PDs) involve extreme personality traits that disrupt daily life, causing significant suffering and functional limitations. Common in health care settings, PDs are linked to poor quality of life, health issues, and premature mortality. Their complex etiology includes genetic and environmental factors. Clinically, PDs vary but share a core issue: difficulty maintaining healthy relationships, impacting interactions with health-care professionals. Previously seen as categorical diseases, the ICD-11 now views PDs dimensionally, based on functional disturbance severity. Medical practitioners often lack insight into PDs, partly because patients seek help for other issues that mask underlying personality problems. PDs negatively influence the clinical presentation and outcomes of other medical conditions, reducing life expectancy. Therefore, greater recognition of PDs in clinical practice is essential. Key Words: ICD-11, personality disorders, personality traits, review article. 15/57: Cycloserine-Induced Psychiatric Manifestations: A Case Series Dr Ipsita Basu Department of psychiatry, junior resident, AFMC, pune, India. Email: quillofhope@gmail.com Introduction: Cycloserine, a second-line antitubercular agent for RR/MDR-TB, can cross the blood-brain barrier and cause neurological and psychiatric issues, especially when combined with isoniazid. Aim: To study psychiatric manifestations in RR/MDR-TB patients treated with cycloserine-based regimens. Methods: Three RR/MDR-TB patients on cycloserine-based treatment were followed from the onset of psychiatric symptoms until resolution. Results: A 28-year-old male experienced severe depression with suicidal ideas, requiring antidepressants, ECT, and cycloserine withdrawal. A 22-year-old male developed psychosis, resolving in 3 days after stopping cycloserine and isoniazid, treated with olanzapine. A 28-year-old female had psychosis, improving within a month after stopping cycloserine and adding risperidone. Conclusion: Young, previously healthy patients developed psychiatric symptoms associated with cycloserine. Baseline psychiatric assessment and vigilance for psychiatric manifestations are necessary, though causality could not be established with only three cases. Email: avipsadash142@gmail.com Introduction: Secondary personality change is a syndrome where personality disturbances arise due to a health condition not classified under mental and behavioral disorders. This case involves a patient with personality changes post-cardiovascular event. Case Presentation : A 32-year-old female presented with irritability, abusive, stealing, and stubborn behavior for 2 years following decompressive cranioplasty for cerebral venous sinus thrombosis with hemorrhagic infarct. Baseline blood tests were normal. MRI showed complete thrombosis in the superior sagittal sinus, left transverse and sigmoid sinus, and left internal jugular vein. She received heparin infusion and sodium valproate for behavioral changes. Sertraline was started but later withdrawn due to bleeding risk. Discussion: Secondary personality change is rare. This case highlights the need for further study on its causes and management difficulties. Early recognition and intervention are crucial for better outcomes. Email: vidhu.arora4@gmail.com Background: Alcohol dependence is a global public health issue linked to physical and psychological problems, including sexual dysfunction. This study examines the impact of alcohol and nicotine dependence on sexual dysfunction in male patients. Method: A cross-sectional study of 103 male participants with Alcohol Dependence Syndrome (ADS) at a tertiary care hospital in Maharashtra, India. Sexual dysfunction was assessed using the Arizona Sexual Experiences Scale (ASEX), and alcohol dependence was evaluated with the Severity of Alcohol Dependence Questionnaire (SAD-Q). Nicotine dependence was measured using the Fagerstrom Nicotine Dependence Scale. Statistical analyses were performed to explore correlations. Results: 54.4% of participants experienced sexual dysfunction, with erectile dysfunction (82.1%), difficulty in sexual arousal (73.2%), and low sexual drive (69.9%) being most common. Higher alcohol and nicotine dependence were significantly correlated with worse sexual health outcomes. Conclusion: Alcohol and nicotine dependence significantly affect sexual health in male patients, highlighting the need for comprehensive treatment addressing both dependencies. Dr. Duthya.R Department of psychiatry, Associate specialist, Tata Main Hospital, Jamshedpur, Jharkhand, India. Email: dyuthy.r@tatasteel.com Introduction: Schizophrenia spectrum disorders are chronic psychiatric illnesses. INSIGHT includes awareness of the mental disorder, understanding social consequences, awareness of treatment needs, specific symptoms, and attributing symptoms to the disorder. Aim: To estimate the proportion of poor insight and determine associated factors in individuals with schizophrenia spectrum disorders. Method: A cross-sectional study of 54 individuals with schizophrenia spectrum disorders collected data on socio-demographic and clinical variables, using various scales including the Modified Kuppuswami’s Socio-Economic Status Scale and Medication Adherence Rating Scale. Results: 63% of individuals had poor insight. Factors significantly associated with poor insight included age, socio-economic status, illness course, current substance use, poor medication adherence, higher PANSS scores, and longer IOWA test duration. Conclusion: Poor insight is common among individuals with schizophrenia spectrum disorders, associated with young age, lower socio-economic status, continuous illness course, substance use, poor medication adherence, higher symptom scores, and poor cognitive functions. Email: anjushaprasanti@gmail.com Introduction: Oral cenesthopathy is a delusional disorder-somatic type marked by abnormal oral sensations without physical findings, often leading to repeated medical consultations. Aims and Objectives: Demonstrate the complexities of diagnosing and managing oral cenesthopathy. Case Report: 44-year-old male experienced abnormal oral sensations for three years, including throat fullness and tongue size alterations, starting during the COVID-19 second wave. Despite multiple evaluations, no physical abnormalities were found. The patient persistently sought consultations, significantly impacting daily functioning and mood. Diagnosed with delusional disorder-somatic type per DSM-5, he was treated with risperidone (4 mg) and sertraline (50 mg) along with psychological and psychosocial management. Conclusion: Diagnosing oral cenesthopathy is challenging as patients often reject psychological explanations. Management typically involves antipsychotics and psychotherapy, requiring interdisciplinary care. The Panic Disorder Severity Scale (PDSS), Hamilton Depression Rating Scale (HAM-D), and Hamilton Anxiety Rating Scale (HAM-A) were measured at baseline and after two weeks. Results Active rTMS significantly reduced PDSS scores at follow-up compared to baseline (p&lt;0.001) and the sham group (p=0.006). HAM-D scores in the active group were also significantly lower than the sham group (p=0.018). Conclusion High-frequency rTMS over the left DLPFC may help treat PD with comorbid depression. Larger studies are needed to confirm its efficacy. While urban areas have better availability of mental health professionals, rural regions remain underserved with barriers such as stigma, lack of awareness, and inadequate infrastructure. Liaison psychiatry, which integrates mental health services into general healthcare, offers a promising solution to bridge this gap. This paper explores the need for liaison psychiatry in rural India, where chronic illnesses often coexist with undiagnosed psychiatric conditions such as depression and anxiety. It highlights current inequities, including a severe shortage of mental health professionals in rural areas, limited training for general practitioners, and sociocultural challenges that impede care delivery. Innovative strategies to expand liaison psychiatry in rural India are discussed, such as task-shifting to train general physicians and community health workers, leveraging telepsychiatry to connect rural populations with urban specialists, and integrating mental health into existing national health programs. Case studies, such as the Atmiyata initiative and NIMHANS telepsychiatry projects, demonstrate successful models of community-based and technology-driven care. Conclusion: The paper emphasizes the importance of policy support, including implementing the Mental Healthcare Act, 2017, in rural contexts, increasing budget allocations for mental health, and fostering public-private partnerships to sustain services. Keywords: Liaison Psychiatry, Rural mental health. 310/877: A comparative study to assess efficacy of repetitive transcranial magnetic stimulation with and without pharmacotherapy in patients of obsessive-compulsive disorder DR. Methods: This study involved 40 OCD patients undergoing rTMS over the supplementary motor area (SMA) for one year. Symptoms were measured using the YBOCS scale at baseline and after 18 sessions of rTMS. Results: YBOCS scores significantly decreased following rTMS treatment. Conclusion: rTMS combined with pharmacotherapy shows a significant difference in YBOCS scores, suggesting enhanced efficacy for treating OCD. A comparative study of prevalence of Psychological Distress Among Rural and Urban Females in the Central India Dr. Prashant Maravi 1 , Dr. Rahul Mathur 1 , Dr. Richa Choudhary 1 , Dr. VS Pal 2 , Dr. Riya Gangwal 3 , Dr. Simran Sandhu 3 Assistant Professor, Department of psychiatry, MGM medical college, Indore HOD and Professor, Department of psychiatry, MGM medical college, Indore Junior Resident, Department of psychiatry, MGM medical college, Indore Background: Mental health disorders are a significant public health concern in India, with the NMHS (2015-16) reporting a lifetime prevalence of 19.33% in urban metros and 12.28% in rural areas. Objective: This study aims to compare the prevalence of psychological distress among rural and urban females attending a secondary healthcare center in central India. Methods: Cross-sectional study was conducted involving 320 female participants, with 160 individuals each from rural and urban areas of central India. Participants were systematically recruited from OPD of a secondary healthcare centre. Data was collected using the Hindi version GHQ-12, threshold score of ≥3 was used to indicate significant psychological distress. Results: 45% of urban females and 30% of rural females scored ≥3 on GHQ-12. Urban females had 1.87 times higher odds of psychological distress compared to rural females. Chi-square analysis revealed a statistically significant difference in prevalence between urban and rural females (χ² = 7.14, p &lt; 0.01). Conclusion: These results highlight the need for targeted mental health interventions and resource allocation, particularly focusing on urban female populations. Keywords: Psychological distress, rural, urban, females, GHQ-12, central India.
